| DL-0127 | 2026-08-10 | Technical Owner | **M9.16-R1 — Payment Engine corrective hardening.** An independent architecture review REJECTED M9.16 with one S0, one S1, one S2 and four lesser findings. All seven are closed: outcome interpretation is now action-aware; a raised provider call becomes `AMBIGUOUS` rather than stranding an attempt; the refund ceiling is arbitrated by a database constraint; the unreachable `PARTIALLY_PAID` state is removed; the ledger exception boundary is narrowed to `BillingError`; an ambiguous attempt is no longer voidable; and the provider-event endpoint is renamed and documented as an internal relay rather than presented as webhook compliance. 28 new tests (161 in the payment suite). | **P-1 (S0) is the one that mattered.** `_settle_attempt` is shared by every operation and interpreted the provider's answer through a single table, so `CANCEL` + `SUCCEEDED` — a provider confirming a **void** — was read as `SUCCEED` and recorded a capture, posting a `payment`/`credit` ledger entry for money never collected. Reproduced before the fix (`attempt=succeeded`, `intent=paid`, `captured=100000`, one ledger entry) and after (`attempt=cancelled`, `captured=0`, **zero ledger entries**). The defect was not a missing check but a **missing input**: the method never knew what had been asked. The correction is declarative rather than conditional — `ACTION_TRIGGERS` selects a table per action, `CANCEL_STATUS_TRIGGERS` maps success to a cancellation, `REFUND_STATUS_TRIGGERS` is empty because a refund outcome moves no attempt state, `action` is a **required keyword argument** so a new call site cannot omit it, and a second independent gate, `asserts_money_moved(outcome, action)`, stands between any outcome and the Ledger. The webhook path reads the action from the operation the key belongs to, so the same defect cannot arrive by that door. **P-2 (S1):** a provider that *raised* skipped phase 3 entirely, leaving the attempt in `started` — open, so retry and cancellation were both refused, and reconciliation ignored it because it only resolved `AMBIGUOUS`; one connection reset made an invoice permanently uncollectable with no operator command able to release it. Every provider call now passes through one method that converts a raised call into `AMBIGUOUS`, the only honest state when we cannot prove the request never left; `ProviderNotSent` is the single exception an adapter may raise to assert nothing was transmitted, and it maps to a local failure. Reconciliation resolves **any** externally-open attempt, not only an ambiguous one. **P-3 (S2):** the refund ceiling was a read-modify-write, so two transactions under *different* request keys each read the same already-refunded total and each passed a ceiling the other was about to consume. `(org_id, attempt_id, sequence)` is now unique, making the second concurrent refund an impossible row until the first commits; the loser re-reads and re-checks against the winner. | [payment-engine.md §12A](../docs/architecture/epic9/payment-engine.md), ADR-0023 PY1–PY9, ADR-0031, M9.0C | **No architecture was redesigned and nothing was weakened.** All thirteen preserved properties hold: the durable pre-call operation claim, its composite primary key, the provider-neutral Foundation, the port, intent/attempt separation, ambiguity as a first-class state, no automatic divergence resolution, no pricing/rating/tax/total/balance computation, the Ledger as owner of financial truth reached only through its own command, immutable settled facts, no instrument data, and Billing absent from Runtime composition. Changes are additive: `ACTION_TRIGGERS`/`CANCEL_STATUS_TRIGGERS`/`REFUND_STATUS_TRIGGERS`/`MONEY_MOVING_ACTIONS` and `ProviderNotSent` in `payment_rules.py`; `resolve_open` and `guard_cancellable` beside the existing services; `RefundSequenceConflict` in `foundation/errors.py` (**not** persistence — the Application must react to it and may not import persistence, which is what decided its home); a `sequence` column and unique constraint on M9.16's own unreleased `cp_billing_payment_refund`. No M9.2–M9.15 surface changed. Two removals, both of unreachable vocabulary: `IntentState.PARTIALLY_PAID` and `IntentTrigger.SETTLE_PARTIAL`, which no code path could produce because every attempt is opened for the whole intent amount — replaced by a test that walks the FSM from `draft` and asserts **every** published state is reachable, so the next dead state cannot be introduced silently. **P-4 was decided rather than implemented.** Option A — an unauthenticated webhook route — was rejected on a security judgement: resolving the organization from provider evidence before authentication requires a cross-tenant lookup, and building one to make an endpoint reachable would put a tenancy hole in the plane that must not have one. The endpoint is renamed `/collections/provider-events/relay/{provider_code}`, summarised `INTERNAL RELAY ONLY`, and its description states that a PSP cannot call it and that **M9.16 must not be read as ADR-0023 PY4 webhook compliance** — asserted by a test, so the honesty cannot drift. One further defect was found while writing the P-1 regression tests: an *ambiguous* attempt was voidable, which would have let a provider confirming a void close an attempt whose money may already have moved; `guard_cancellable` now refuses it. Concurrency is proven by two independent Applications over two independent connections to one file-backed database, with explicit interleaving so the database arbitrates deterministically rather than a thread schedule that may not overlap. | Accepted |
| DL-0128 | 2026-08-10 | Technical Owner | **M9.17 — Dunning & Collections Engine.** Recovery policy across all four Billing layers plus a Studio UI: immutable versioned strategies, one case per obligation, durable actions decided before anything leaves the building, append-only execution evidence, and a decision trail that replays after the strategy is rewritten. 12 commands, 15 queries, 6 tables via migration 0027, 25 paths under `/recovery`, 10 Studio screens, 97 tests, 3859/3859 repository-wide, ruff clean. | **Collections is a policy engine, not a payment engine — it decides whether to ask; Payments moves the money; Ledger records the fact.** Two decisions carried the milestone. First, **there is no money in this domain at all**: no amount, no balance, no `outstanding`, no copied invoice total — not as a column, a field, or a wire field. An AST scan bans the identifiers across all eight files and a second bans even the `Money` *type*, because a domain that can hold one eventually stores one, and a stored figure is a second authority on a number the Ledger owns that will be the stale one on the day it matters. Second, the **M9.16-R1 invariant is enforced three times in differently-shaped ways**: a blacklist branch that short-circuits on `AMBIGUOUS` before the policy is consulted at all; a **whitelist** gate so a payment signal added later defaults to *refusing* a money request rather than permitting one; and a third check at the moment of execution, which is the only one that can see an action planned an hour ago against a failed payment while a reconciliation has since moved that attempt back to ambiguous. Five payment attempt states map to `AMBIGUOUS` and an unrecognised state does too — a state the engine cannot vouch for is one whose safety it cannot vouch for. Policy is **immutable and versioned** with no update path on the repository, and a case reads the version *it* names rather than today's, which is the whole of decision reproducibility in one method. | [collections-engine.md](../docs/architecture/epic9/collections-engine.md), ADR-0023, ADR-0021, ADR-0031, M9.0C, DL-0127 | **M9.2's dunning surface is untouched.** `DunningState`, `DunningTrigger`, `DunningPolicy`, `DunningProcess`, `DunningRepository` and `cp_billing_dunning` keep every member, field and row, asserted by `test_m9_2_dunning_is_untouched`; the richer aggregates land beside them as M9.13–M9.16's did beside theirs (§A12). Its three `billing.dunning.*` event types are **reused rather than renamed** — a case opening *is* dunning starting, and a second type would give one business fact two names (AR-2). The router took **`/recovery`**, not `/collections`: that prefix is M9.16's, and two routers under one prefix shadow each other by registration order, silently. Concurrency is proven with two independent Applications over two independent connections to one file-backed database — all six mandated scenarios, `CaseStale` declared in `foundation/errors.py` rather than persistence for the same layering reason as `RefundSequenceConflict`. **Four defects were found and fixed during implementation.** The serious one, D-3, was an **unbounded dunning loop**: a failed action did not advance the stage, so a failed payment retry re-planned the same rung forever — the first end-to-end drain ran fourteen rounds without leaving stage 1, which in production is a customer chased without end and a strategy that can never exhaust. A failed action now climbs the rung (we tried what the stage asked and it did not work — a completed step), only `DEFERRED`/`NOT_SENT` hold it, and the decision row acts as a memo so a repeated evaluation reuses the action it names rather than planning another. D-2 was an M9.16-R1 P-5 repeat caught before review: `ACTION_DUE` and `IN_PROGRESS` were published but unreachable, now fixed and guarded by a reachability walk. **Billing runs no scheduler** — what is due is a query, and whoever polls it lives outside this plane, which means an unattended deployment collects nothing. ADR-0023 is not amended and M9.16-R1's P-4 webhook limitation is unchanged. | Accepted |
